\vspace{0.7mm}
\begin{contextbox}
  \footnotesize
  \labeltext{Area of interest.}
  My research interests include pressure management in drinking-water distribution systems, particularly pressure behavior at remote critical nodes. I have previously worked on this problem using field measurements and model-based control methods, as described in a \href{https://ascelibrary.org/doi/10.1061/JWRMD5.WRENG-7321}{\textit{published case study}}. For this project, I would like to approach a related problem from a machine-learning perspective: whether supervised learning can use current and historical SCADA measurements to predict future pressure at a monitored location.
\end{contextbox}

\vspace{0.8mm}
\begin{rationalebox}
  \footnotesize
  \labeltext{Dataset selection rationale.}
  I selected BattLeDIM and BATADAL not only because copies are available on Kaggle, but because both are established research benchmarks with identifiable official sources. Their repositories provide network models, event information, benchmark documentation, reproducibility resources, and peer-reviewed studies. This lets me verify the data independently of Kaggle and trace previous work, which will be useful in Milestone 2 for identifying a predictive task that extends rather than simply repeats prior results.
\end{rationalebox}

\vspace{1.2mm}
{\color{Navy}\bfseries\large Candidates at a glance}\par
\vspace{0.6mm}
\scriptsize
\rowcolors{2}{PaleBlue}{white}
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\bfseries\color{Navy}\raggedright\arraybackslash}m{1.10in}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}m{2.87in}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}m{2.87in}@{}}
  \rowcolor{Blue!13}
  & \multicolumn{1}{c}{\textbf{BattLeDIM: L-Town}} & \multicolumn{1}{c}{\textbf{BATADAL: C-Town}} \\
  Sampling & 5 minutes & 1 hour \\
  Scale & 210,240 time observations & 15,027 records \\
  Main data & Pressure, flow, tank level, demand & Pressure, flow, tank levels, actuator states \\
  Proposed target & Node pressure 15 minutes ahead & Node pressure 1 hour ahead \\
  Research support & Official archive + DOI + models + benchmark literature & Official site + model + event records + benchmark literature \\
\end{tabular}
\normalsize

\vspace{0.8mm}
\begin{datasetbox}[Teal]{Candidate 1 \quad BattLeDIM: L-Town}
  \footnotesize
  \begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.88in}X@{}}
    \labeltext{Dataset} & BattLeDIM is an academic benchmark for the virtual L-Town drinking-water distribution network. Its 2018--2019 SCADA data contain 119 numerical channels: 33 pressures, three flows, one tank level, and 82 automated meter readings. Network topology and leakage-event information are also available; all main features are listed on page 2. \\
    \labeltext{My proposed task} & I would predict pressure at a selected monitored node 15 minutes ahead using current and past pressure, flow, tank level, consumption, and time information. Since the target is a continuous pressure value, this would be a regression problem. \\
    \labeltext{Why I chose it} & Its five-minute distributed measurements are closely related to critical-node pressure management. Beyond Kaggle, the official Zenodo archive provides a DOI, network models and event data, while the project site links evaluation resources, the benchmark paper, and a special collection of related studies. This makes provenance and prior work traceable. I would study pressure forecasting rather than repeat the original leakage-detection task, with leakage periods retained for a possible robustness analysis. \\
    \labeltext{Sources} & \scriptsize \href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/larserikrisholm/battledim}{Kaggle copy}\sepmark \href{https://zenodo.org/records/4017659}{Official Zenodo dataset}\sepmark \href{https://battledim.ucy.ac.cy/}{Benchmark site}\sepmark \href{https://doi.org/10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001601}{Benchmark paper} \\
  \end{tabularx}
\end{datasetbox}

\vspace{0.6mm}
\begin{datasetbox}[Gold]{Candidate 2 \quad BATADAL: C-Town}
  \footnotesize
  \begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.88in}X@{}}
    \labeltext{Dataset} & BATADAL is a documented benchmark for the simulated C-Town water distribution network. Its hourly SCADA data contain 43 variables: seven tank levels, 12 flows, 12 discrete actuator states, and 12 pressures. The files contain 8,761 normal-operation, 4,177 development, and 2,089 test records; all main features are listed on page 2. \\
    \labeltext{My proposed task} & I would predict pressure at a selected monitored location one hour ahead using current and past tank levels, pump and valve states, flows, and pressures. Attack labels would not be inputs or the main target. \\
    \labeltext{Why I chose it} & BATADAL was selected because it combines hydraulic measurements with explicit pump and valve states, allowing investigation of how system operation relates to future pressure. Beyond the Kaggle copy, the official benchmark site provides the original datasets, attack-event records, the C-Town EPANET model, challenge rules, and the peer-reviewed paper documenting the benchmark and submitted approaches. These resources make the dataset independently verifiable and make previous work traceable, which is valuable for identifying a distinct supervised learning task. The proposed project would focus on pressure forecasting rather than repeat attack detection, while attack periods could later support a robustness test. \\
    \labeltext{Sources} & \scriptsize \href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/minhbtnguyen/batadal-a-dataset-for-cyber-attack-detection}{Kaggle copy}\sepmark \href{https://www.batadal.net/data.html}{Official datasets}\sepmark \href{https://www.batadal.net/challenge.html}{Benchmark materials}\sepmark \href{https://doi.org/10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0000969}{Benchmark paper} \\
  \end{tabularx}
\end{datasetbox}

The full set of official rules is available as PDF:\\nL17: \\nL18: cite7†Official Rules Document L19: ### Problem Setting\\nL20: C-Town Public Utility (CPU) is the main water distribution system operator of C-Town (Figure 1). For many years, CPU has operated a static distribution topology. In the last year, CPU has introduced novel smart technology to enable remote data collection from sensors in the field, and remote control of actuators. Shortly after that new technology has been introduced, anomalous low levels in Tank T5 and high levels in Tank T1 were observed. A month later, a water overflow in Tank T1 occurred.\\nL21: While CPU personnel at the control center were able to see the anomalous readings for the first two episodes, Tank T1 overflow took place unexpectedly while the water level readings were always below the alarm thresholds and pumping operations appeared to be normal. Searching for the causes, CPU engineers suspect potential cyber-attacks for all these episodes.\\nL22: In particular, they are considering adversaries that are able to activate and deactivate the actuators in C-Town, as well as altering the readings of the sensors deployed in the network and the reported status of actuators, and interfering with the connections established between networked components. The participants' task is thus to develop an online alert system for cyber-physical attacks.\\nL23: cite8†Image Figure 1: Graphical representation of C-Town water distribution system..\\nL24: \\nL25: cite2†Datasets L26: ### Goal of the attack detection algorithms\\nL27: The primary goal of the detection mechanism is to reliably detect the presence of an ongoing attack from the SCADA readings, and to do so in the shortest amount of time. In addition, the algorithm should avoid false alarms and recognize when the threat is no longer in place. Due to the distributed nature of the WDS, an ideal detection mechanism should also be able to identify which components of the physical network are being attacked in order to facilitate and hasten incident resolution.\\nL28: Furthermore, the inherent interdependence of the elements in the water network should theoretically allow for the detection of anomalies even when the adversary tries to conceal his actions by altering the SCADA readings of one or a few deployed sensors. The control system will operate in the control room, based on the available SCADA data. Further information is detailed in the rules document.\\nL29: ### Test data and evaluation criteria\\nL30: A test dataset will be made available for a short time window (cite3†see schedule ), during which the participants are required to run their algorithms and submit a detection report (see rules document for further details). The test dataset will contain a few months of data and contain attack instances that may differ from those of the development dataset. All algorithms will then be compared by adopting quantitative criteria based on the time-to-detection and events classification (confusion matrix).\\nL31: ### References\\nL32:   * R. Taormina, S. Galelli, N. O. Tippenhauer, E. Salomons, and A. Ostfeld (2017). \\\"Characterizing Cyber-Physical Attacks On Water Distribution Systems\\\" J. Water Resour. Plann. \\\\end{minipage}\\r\\n\\\\end{tcolorbox}\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\vspace{0.7mm}\\r\\n\\\\begin{contextbox}\\r\\n  \\\\footnotesize\\r\\n  \\\\labeltext{Area of interest.}\\r\\n  My research interests include pressure management in drinking-water distribution systems, particularly pressure behavior at remote critical nodes. I have previously worked on this problem using field measurements and model-based control methods, as described in a \\\\href{https://ascelibrary.org/doi/10.1061/JWRMD5.WRENG-7321}{\\\\textit{published case study}}. For this project, I would like to approach a related problem from a machine-learning perspective: whether supervised learning can use current and historical SCADA measurements to predict future pressure at a monitored location.\\r\\n\\\\end{contextbox}\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\vspace{0.8mm}\\r\\n\\\\begin{rationalebox}\\r\\n  \\\\footnotesize\\r\\n  \\\\labeltext{Dataset selection rationale.}\\r\\n  I selected BattLeDIM and BATADAL not only because copies are available on Kaggle, but because both are established research benchmarks with identifiable official sources. This lets me verify the data independently of Kaggle and trace previous work, which will be useful in Milestone 2 for identifying a predictive task that extends rather than simply repeats prior results.\\r\\n\\\\end{rationalebox}\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\vspace{1.2mm}\\r\\n{\\\\color{Navy}\\\\bfseries\\\\large Candidates at a glance}\\\\par\\r\\n\\\\vspace{0.6mm}\\r\\n\\\\scriptsize\\r\\n\\\\rowcolors{2}{PaleBlue}{white}\\r\\n\\\\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\\\\bfseries\\\\color{Navy}\\\\raggedright\\\\arraybackslash}m{1.10in}>{\\\\raggedright\\\\arraybackslash}m{2.87in}>{\\\\raggedright\\\\arraybackslash}m{2.87in}@{}}\\r\\n  \\\\rowcolor{Blue!13}\\r\\n  & \\\\multicolumn{1}{c}{\\\\textbf{BattLeDIM: L-Town}} & \\\\multicolumn{1}{c}{\\\\textbf{BATADAL: C-Town}} \\\\\\\\\\r\\n  Sampling & 5 minutes & 1 hour \\\\\\\\\\r\\n  Scale & 210,240 time observations & 15,027 records \\\\\\\\\\r\\n  Main data & Pressure, flow, tank level, demand & Pressure, flow, tank levels, actuator states \\\\\\\\\\r\\n  Proposed target & Node pressure 15 minutes ahead & Node pressure 1 hour ahead \\\\\\\\\\r\\n  Research support & Official archive + DOI + models + benchmark literature & Official site + model + event records + benchmark literature \\\\\\\\\\r\\n\\\\end{tabular}\\r\\n\\\\normalsize\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\vspace{0.8mm}\\r\\n\\\\begin{datasetbox}[Teal]{Candidate 1 \\\\quad BattLeDIM: L-Town}\\r\\n  \\\\footnotesize\\r\\n  \\\\begin{tabularx}{\\\\linewidth}{@{}>{\\\\raggedright\\\\arraybackslash}p{0.88in}X@{}}\\r\\n    \\\\labeltext{Dataset} & BattLeDIM is an academic benchmark for the virtual L-Town drinking-water distribution network. Its 2018--2019 SCADA data contain 119 numerical channels: 33 pressures, three flows, one tank level, and 82 automated meter readings. Network topology and leakage-event information are also available; all main features are listed on page 2. \\\\\\\\\\r\\n    \\\\labeltext{My proposed task} & I would predict pressure at a selected monitored node 15 minutes ahead using current and past pressure, flow, tank level, consumption, and time information. Since the target is a continuous pressure value, this would be a regression problem. \\\\\\\\\\r\\n    \\\\labeltext{Why I chose it} & Its five-minute distributed measurements are closely related to critical-node pressure management. Beyond Kaggle, the official Zenodo archive provides a DOI, network models and event data, while the project site links evaluation resources, the benchmark paper, and a special collection of related studies. This makes provenance and prior work traceable. I would study pressure forecasting rather than repeat the original leakage-detection task, with leakage periods retained for a possible robustness analysis. \\\\\\\\\\r\\n    \\\\labeltext{Sources} & \\\\scriptsize \\\\href{https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/larserikrisholm/battledim}{Kaggle copy}\\\\sepmark \\\\href{https://zenodo.org/records/4017659}{Official Zenodo dataset}\\\\sepmark \\\\href{https://battledim.ucy.ac.cy/}{Benchmark site}\\\\sepmark \\\\href{https://doi.org/10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001601}{Benchmark paper} \\\\\\\\\\r\\n  \\\\end{tabularx}\\r\\n\\\\end{datasetbox}\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\vspace{0.6mm}\\r\\n\\\\begin{datasetbox}[Gold]{Candidate 2 \\\\quad BATADAL: C-Town}\\r\\n  \\\\footnotesize\\r\\n  \\\\begin{tabularx}{\\\\linewidth}{@{}>{\\\\raggedright\\\\arraybackslash}p{0.88in}X@{}}\\r\\n    \\\\labeltext{Dataset} & BATADAL is a documented benchmark for the simulated C-Town water distribution network. Course Description   [From the NJIT catalog]: This course is an introduction to machine learning and contains both theory and applications. Students will get exposure to a broad range of machine learning methods and hands-on practice on real data. Topics include Bayesian classification, perceptron, neural networks, logistic regression, support vector machines, decision trees, random forests, boosting, dimensionality reduction, unsupervised learning, regression, and learning new feature spaces. There will be several programming assignments, one course project, one mid-term and one final exam. 

PAGE 2
 [Instructor’s description]: Machine Learning (ML) develops computer programs that can improve their performance by tapping into existing data and taking feedback from the environment. Systems based on ML have already exceeded human performance in several tasks, including image medical image classification and games like Chess and Go. ML has also made leaps in even more complex tasks, such as Natural Language Processing or self-driving vehicles, and  generating text, code images, video and music almost indistinguishable from that produced by human experts and artists. This course offers an intensive introduction to the fundamental ML concepts and algorithms that constitute the core of these spectacular developments. It takes you on a tour from the basic mathematical notions and algorithms to the foundations of recent developments in Deep Learning (DL). You will gain exposure to cutting-edge ML development tools such as Scikit-learn and PyTorch via hands-on assignments and projects that will instill a working and immediately applicable knowledge of ML methods, and which will prepare you for more advanced courses in ML and DL.   Prerequisites  This course does not have other courses as prerequisites.  Background on basic calculus, linear algebra, probability and statistics, and Python programming is required. Familiarity with the following mathematical concepts and their notation is essential for success in this course.  Calculus Linear Algebra Probability and Statistics Basic notion of derivatives Differentiation of         polynomials,         exponentials,         logarithms Product rule Quotient rule Chain rule Partial differentiation Multivariate chain rule Gradient Basic notion of integrals Fundamental Theorem         of Calculus 
Vectors and vector notation Lines, planes, hyperplanes Normal vectors Vector norm Dot product Orthogonality and projection Linear transformation Matrix notation Matrix addition & multiplication Matrix transpose, inverse Matrix rank Eigenvalues and eigenvectors* Basis vectors* Eigendecomposition* 
Basic probability and selection Conditional probability & independence Bayes' theorem* Maximum likelihood* Random variables Expectation Mean, variance, deviation Error, bias Standardization Distributions and sampling Probability density function Cumulative distribution function Uniform distribution Normal distribution (Gaussian)  * = A review of this topic will be presented for those who have not encountered it before.  The following free online materials are recommended for reviewing this background:  ● Mathematics for Machine Learning ● A visual guide to NumPy

Friedman — Springer,  ISBN-13: 978-0387848570   Learning Outcomes  By the end of the course, you will be able to: a.      Identify the main types of Machine Learning (ML). b.      Evaluate the quality of online resources related to ML. c.      Recognize problems amenable to ML methods. d.      Describe and explain a wide variety of ML algorithms. e.      Apply various ML algorithms in novel situations. f.       Evaluate the performance of ML models. g.      Modify ML models in order to improve their performance. h.      Adapt ML algorithms and models to the given data and application.

[Outcomes: a,d]  Programming Assignments [25%] Six hands-on Machine Learning programming assignments. [Outcomes: c,e-h]  Mini-Project [25%] An implementation project in Machine Learning consisting of four milestones with the following weights: [3%, 6%, 1%, 15%]. [Outcomes: b,c,e]  Professional Quiz (Mid-Semester) [10%] A 90-minute online quiz of the style often seen by ML professionals in a job interview, covering all material in Weeks 1-7. Students are allowed to bring at most 5 letter-sized pages of notes prepared solely by themselves. These must be uploaded to Canvas beforehand. The quiz will be on Canvas with proctoring using Respondus LockDown browser with video recording. Students must ensure that their computer is equipped with a working webcam. Students must ensure that their computing equipment is fully-functional and fully-charged. [Outcomes: a,d,e]  Professional Quiz (End-of-Semester) [20%] Covers the entire course, but with greater emphasis on the material in Weeks 7-14. 120 minutes. In other respects, similar to the Mid-Semester Quiz. [Outcomes: a,d,e]

Grading Policies  Letter Grades  In accordance with the graduate grade legend, the raw total percentage assessment score will be converted to a final letter grade that will appear on your transcript. The conversion table for this course is:  Letter Grade Percentage Range A   B+ B   C+ C F 
90  —  100 80  —  <90 70  —  <80 65  —  <70 60  —  <65             <60  In very exceptional cases the letter grade may be raised one level higher than what is suggested by the above conversion table. Examples include a top score in the proctored end-of-semester Professional Quiz, or a Mini-Project with very significant novel contributions.  Incomplete A grade of I (incomplete) is given in rare cases where work cannot be completed during the semester due to documented long-term illness or unexpected absence for other serious reasons. A student requesting special consideration should be in good standing (i.e. with a passing grade on 

PAGE 7
coursework submitted before the absence). When special consideration is granted, the student receives a provisional I if there is no other way to make up for the documented lost time; in such cases, an email with a timeline for makeup work will be sent to the student. Note that according to NJIT regulations, an I must always be resolved by the end of the following semester.  Late Submission Policy Generally speaking, assignments, project milestones, and participation exercises will be accepted late without penalty, but only up until the time grading has begun, or solutions or other feedback have been released to members of the class. At that time, the class will be informed that submissions have closed, that no further submissions will be accepted, and that any missing work will be given a mark of zero. Students should be aware that grades, solutions, and/or feedback may be released at any time after the deadline, at the sole discretion of the lecturer and without prior warning. The only way to ensure that work will be accepted without penalty is to submit it before the deadline. No individual extensions will be granted. However, special consideration may be given in rare cases when a student is unable to complete an assignment for serious, unavoidable reasons — these must be communicated and documented promptly.  Grading Feedback Assignment marks will be accompanied with solutions and/or general feedback summarizing common mistakes. Individual grading feedback will be given where appropriate. Further clarifications can be provided by contacting the instructor and/or the course grader.  Grade Corrections Check the grades in course work and report errors promptly. Generative AI Tools and Other External Resources Sometimes you may come across code, text or other helpful information online, or you may be able to generate it using AI tools such as ChatGPT or other Large Language Models (LLMs). In most cases, you will be allowed to integrate this information into your solution. However, if you do, you must always give the appropriate credit and citations (e.g. links) for the material you use (especially when you use the code and text you found online). In the case you use an LLM, you must say that you did so, and present the entire transcript of your ‘conversation’ with it, which should show what you asked and how you guided it, or were guided by it to the delivered solution. Your ‘conversation’ with it must be entirely yours, and sufficiently different from that of other students. Failure to give appropriate credit when using the work of others (whether human or AI) is considered plagiarism, and may lead to disciplinary action under NJIT's Academic Integrity policy (see below). KIOS Research and Innovation Center of Excellence\nL21:   * 2. Delft University of Technology\nL22:   * 3. Technion – Israel Institute of Technology\nL23:   * 4. Tsinghua University\nL24: ## Description\nL25: Drinking Water Distribution Networks (DWDN) are susceptible to infrastructure failures, which may lead to water losses. Typically, these water losses are due to background leakages and pipe bursts which may occur anywhere within the distribution network. Background leakages are normally difficult to detect due to their small size, whereas pipe bursts are easier to locate as they are of larger size and may appear on the surface.\nL26: The early detection and localization of some leakage event is extremely important, as this would reduce the time required for accommodating the event and therefore reducing the risk of further infrastructure degradation, contamination events and consumer complaints.\nL27: In previous years, a number of methodologies have been proposed to detect and isolate the location of leakage events using various types of sensor measurements. These methods were commonly evaluated on private commercial datasets, and as a result, it is not possible to objectively compare these methods in their ability to detect and isolate leaks.\nL28: In the past year, a leakage detection dataset has been proposed, LeakDB, based on benchmark networks and created using the WNTR tool, using pressure-driven demands and realistic leakage modelling. Inspired by the “BATtle of the Attack Detection ALgorithms” (BATADAL), which focused on the detection of cyber-physical attacks, our team decided to organize a similar “battle” focusing on leakage events.\nL29: The Battle of the Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods (BattLeDIM),  aims at objectively comparing the performance of methods for the detection and localization of leakage events, relying on SCADA measurements of flow and pressure sensors installed within water distribution networks. Participants may use different types of tools and methods, including (but not limited to) engineering judgement, machine learning, statistical methods, signal processing, and model-based fault diagnosis approaches.\nL30: ## Files\nL31: \nL32: ###\nL33: \nL34: 2018_Fixed_Leakages_Report.txt\nL35: \nL36: cite11†Preview L37: ###\nL38: \nL39: Files (550.5 MB)\nL40: Name  | Size  |\nL41: --- Incomplete A grade of I (incomplete) is given in rare cases where work cannot be completed during the semester due to documented long-term illness or unexpected absence for other serious reasons. A student requesting special consideration should be in good standing (i.e. with a passing grade on \\r\\n\\r\\nPAGE 7\\r\\ncoursework submitted before the absence). Late Submission Policy Generally speaking, assignments, project milestones, and participation exercises will be accepted late without penalty, but only up until the time grading has begun, or solutions or other feedback have been released to members of the class. At that time, the class will be informed that submissions have closed, that no further submissions will be accepted, and that any missing work will be given a mark of zero. Students should be aware that grades, solutions, and/or feedback may be released at any time after the deadline, at the sole discretion of the lecturer and without prior warning. The only way to ensure that work will be accepted without penalty is to submit it before the deadline. No individual extensions will be granted. However, special consideration may be given in rare cases when a student is unable to complete an assignment for serious, unavoidable reasons — these must be communicated and documented promptly. Generative AI Tools and Other External Resources Sometimes you may come across code, text or other helpful information online, or you may be able to generate it using AI tools such as ChatGPT or other Large Language Models (LLMs). # Metadata for BattLeDIM 2020 Dataset (Website: http://battledim.ucy.ac.cy/)


# File contents description:
"L-TOWN.inp" EPANET Model (INP): 
A model of the network is provided with nominal parameters for all the system elements. 
The nominal base demands for each node are based on average historical metered consumption. 
In general, the difference between the actual and the nominal values for each consumer type (residential and commercial)is less than 10%. 
Weekly demand profiles for two consumer types(residential and commercial) are also provided, however they do not capture the yearly seasonality. Furthermore, the EPANET model parameters may be different from the actual network parameters (e.g., diameters, roughness coefficients), and it is assumed than in general this difference is no greater than 10% of the nominal values.

"L-TOWN_Real.inp" EPANET Model (INP): 
The model of the network used to generate the datasets. 
Contains the real network parameters and consumer demands.
It does not contain any leakages.

"dataset_configuration.yalm" :
Configuration file for generating the Historical (2018) and Evaluation (2019) datasets of the BattLeDIM competition.
Contains:
-file name of network
-dataset start and end times
-leakage information (link ID, start Time, end Time, leak Diameter (m), leak Type, peak Time)
-sensor locations (link or node IDs)

"2018_SCADA_Demands.csv" : 
Part of the Historical Dataset (year 2018) of the BattLeDIM competition.
Water demand flow measurements from 82 Automated Meter Reading devices in the L-Town network. 
Measurement units: Liters per hour (L/h). 
Column headings correspond to Node IDs in the L-Town.inp network file. 

"2018_SCADA_Flows.csv" : 
Part of the Historical Dataset (year 2018) of the BattLeDIM competition.
Flow measurements from 3 flow sensors in the L-Town network.
Measurement units: Cubic meters per hour(m^3/h). 
Column headings correspond to Link IDs in the L-Town.inp network file. 

"2018_SCADA_Levels.csv" :
Part of the Historical Dataset (year 2018) of the BattLeDIM competition.
Tank level measurements from 1 level sensor in the L-Town network
Measurement units: Meters (m). 
Column headings correspond to Tank Node IDs in the L-Town.inp network file. 

"2018_SCADA_Pressures.csv" :
Part of the Historical Dataset (year 2018) of the BattLeDIM competition.
Pressure measurements from 33 pressure sensors in the L-Town network.
Measurement units: Meters (m). 
Column headings correspond to Node IDs in the L-Town.inp network file. 

"2018_SCADA.xlsx" :
Part of the Historical Dataset (year 2018) of the BattLeDIM competition.
Contains all the information of "2018_SCADA_Demands.csv", "2018_SCADA_Flows.csv", "2018_SCADA_Levels.csv" and "2018_SCADA_Pressures.csv" in compact .xlsx form.

"2018_Fixed_Leakages_Report.txt": 
Part of the Historical Dataset (year 2018) of the BattLeDIM competition.
The repair times of pipe bursts that have been fixed in the 2018 dataset are provided.

"2018_Leakages":
Timeseries of all leakages in the Historical Dataset (year 2018) of the BattLeDIM competition.
Measurement units: Cubic meters per hour(m^3/h). 
Column headings correspond to Link IDs in the L-Town.inp network file. 

"2019_SCADA_Demands.csv" : 
Part of the Evaluation Dataset (year 2019) of the BattLeDIM competition.
Water demand flow measurements from 82 Automated Meter Reading devices in the L-Town network. 
Measurement units: Liters per hour (L/h). 
Column headings correspond to Node IDs in the L-Town.inp network file. 

"2019_SCADA_Flows.csv" : 
Part of the Evaluation Dataset (year 2019) of the BattLeDIM competition.
Flow measurements from 3 flow sensors in the L-Town network.
Measurement units: Cubic meters per hour(m^3/h). 
Column headings correspond to Link IDs in the L-Town.inp network file. 

"2019_SCADA_Levels.csv" :
Part of the Evaluation Dataset (year 2019) of the BattLeDIM competition.
Tank level measurements from 1 level sensor in the L-Town network
Measurement units: Meters (m). 
Column headings correspond to Tank Node IDs in the L-Town.inp network file. 

"2019_SCADA_Pressures.csv" :
Part of the Evaluation Dataset (year 2019) of the BattLeDIM competition.
Pressure measurements from 33 pressure sensors in the L-Town network.
Measurement units: Meters (m). 
Column headings correspond to Node IDs in the L-Town.inp network file. 

"2019_SCADA.xlsx" :
Part of the Evaluation Dataset (year 2019) of the BattLeDIM competition.
Contains all the information of "2019_SCADA_Demands.csv", "2019_SCADA_Flows.csv", "2019_SCADA_Levels.csv" and "2019_SCADA_Pressures.csv" in compact .xlsx form.

Eliades KIOS Research and Innovation Center of Excellence, University of Cyprus, Cyprus\nL7@P0: Riccardo Taormina Technical University Delft, the Netherlands\nL8@P0: Avi Ostfeld Technion – Israel Institute of Technology, Israel\nL9@P0: Zoran Kapelan Technical University Delft, the Netherlands\nL10@P0: Shuming Liu Tsinghua University, China\nL11@P0: Marios Kyriakou KIOS Research and Innovation Center of Excellence, University of Cyprus, Cyprus\nL12@P0: Pavlos Pavlou KIOS Research and Innovation Center of Excellence, University of Cyprus, Cyprus\nL13@P0: Mengning Qiu Technion – Israel Institute of Technology, Israel\nL14@P0: Marios M. Polycarpou KIOS Research and Innovation Center of Excellence, University of Cyprus, Cyprus\nL15@P0: 1. INTRODUCTION\nL16@P0: Drinking Water Distribution Networks (DWDN) are susceptible to infrastructure failures, which may lead to\nL17@P0: water losses. Typically, these water losses are due to background leakages and pipe bursts which may occur\nL18@P0: anywhere within the distribution network. Background leakages are normally difficult to detect due to their\nL19@P0: small size, whereas pipe bursts are easier to locate as they are of larger size and may appear on the surface.\nL20@P0: The early detection and localization of some leakage event is extremely important, as this would reduce the\nL21@P0: time required for accommodating the event and therefore reducing the risk of further infrastructure degrada\u0002tion, contamination events and consumer complaints. In previous years, a number of methodologies have\nL22@P0: been proposed to detect and isolate the location of leakage events using various types of sensor measure\u0002ments. These methods were commonly evaluated on private commercial datasets, and as a result, it is not\nL23@P0: possible to objectively compare these methods in their ability to detect and isolate leaks. In the past year, a\nL24@P0: leakage detection dataset has been proposed, LeakDB [1], based on benchmark networks and created using\nL25@P0: the WNTR tool [2], using pressure-driven demands and realistic leakage modelling [3]. Inspired by the “BATtle\nL26@P0: of the Attack Detection ALgorithms” (BATADAL), which focused on the detection of cyber-physical attacks [4],\nL27@P0: our team decided to organize a similar “battle” focusing on leakage events.\nL28@P0: The Battle of the Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods (BattLeDIM), organized as part of the 2nd Inter\u0002national CCWI/WDSA Joint Conference in Beijing, China (http://www.ccwi-wdsa2020.com/), aims at objec\u0002tively comparing the performance of methods for the detection and localization of leakage events, relying on\nL29@P0: SCADA measurements of flow and pressure sensors installed within water distribution networks. Participants\nL30@P0: may use different types of tools and methods, including (but not limited to) engineering judgement, machine\nL31@P0-1: learning, statistical methods, signal processing, and model-based fault diagnosis approaches.2. CHALLENGE DESCRIPTION\nL32@P1: L-Town (see Fig. 1) is a small hypothetical town with a population of around 10,000 people. The water utility\nL33@P1: of L-Town is responsible for delivering drinking water to consumers through a network of pipes with a total\nL34@P1: length of 42.6km. In previous years, the utility was experiencing a large number of pipe breaks and water\nL35@P1: losses, affecting its service quality. The water distribution network of L-Town is receiving water from two (2)\nL36@P1: reservoirs, and the water utility aims at providing water with a pressure head of at least 20m to all of its\nL37@P1: consumers. A Pressure Reduction Valve (PRV) is installed in the lower part of the town (“Area B”), to help\nL38@P1: reducing background leakages. PRVs are also installed downstream of the two main reservoirs, to help regu\u0002lating the pressure. A pump and a water tank have been installed in the higher part of the town (“Area C”), to\nL39@P1: provide sufficient pressure to the consumers of that area. The tank has a diameter of 16 meters with a cylin\u0002drical shape. The pump has been programmed so that the tank should be refilling during the night and emp\u0002tying to “Area C” during the day. There are three consumer types in L-Town: residential, commercial and in\u0002dustrial. During workdays (Monday to Friday), water consumption follows a similar pattern, whereas during\nL40@P1: the weekend (Saturday and Sunday), there is higher consumption during late hours as the result of night life.\nL41@P1: Areas with industrial users do not follow the same pattern of consumption. L-Town is located in the Northern\nL42@P1: hemisphere, thus higher water usage is expected around July/August, and lower in December/January. For\nL43@P1: the purposes of this challenge, there are no significant variations of water consumption during holidays or\nL44@P1: other special days.\nL45@P1: Figure 1: The L-Town Benchmark Network\nL46@P1: To enhance its capability in monitoring water losses, the water utility of L-Town decided to install one (1) tank\nL47@P1: water level sensor, three (3) flow sensors, and 33 pressure sensors (see Fig. 2), all transmitting their measure\u0002ments every 5 minutes to the utility’s Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition (SCADA) System. Pressure\nL48@P1-2: sensors give an average value of the last 5 minutes, which mitigatesthe uncertainty due to pressure transients in the system. In addition, 82 Automated Metered Readings (AMRs) have been installed in “Area C”, for deliv\u0002ering water consumption data directly to the SCADA system (see Fig. 3). Each AMR gives the aggregated con\u0002sumption of many users in the AMR area.\nL49@P2: During 2018, a number of leakage events occurred, which were detected and fixed by the water utility. How\u0002ever, it is believed that a number of smaller leakages occurred but not revealed. It is also assumed that some\nL50@P2: leakages occurred abruptly, whereas others developed gradually, as incipient events, from background leaks\nL51@P2: into pipe bursts.\nL52@P2: To assist the L-Town water utility decision-making process, the utility developed an EPANET-based nominal\nL53@P2: model of the distribution network, in which base demands were assigned to nodes, following historical data\nL54@P2: of proximity consumers. Moreover, two nominal demand patterns were identified for residential and com\u0002mercial consumer types (with some discrepancies). The utility believes that there might be some inaccuracies\nL55@P2: in the model, e.g., with respect to the pipe roughness and pipe diameters. In addition, the utility was not able\nL56@P2: to confirm the status of all the valves in the network (i.e., whether they are open or closed).\nL57@P2: The L-Town water utility is searching for a solution to help them analyzing the SCADA dataset, and detect\nL58@P2: leakage events as fast as possible. In addition, it is crucial for the utility to have an indication where approxi\u0002mately the leakage occurs, so that the field workers can inspect those potential leaks using their equipment.\nL59@P2-3: Figure 2: The locations of the 33 pressure sensors placed in L-Town are indicated with yellow color along with their node ID.Figure 3: 82 AMR locations (nodes with red color) in “Area C” of L-Town. Nodes colored in blue do not have AMRs installed.\nL60@P3: 3. COMPETITION GOAL\nL61@P3: The L-Town utility has created an open call for teams to demonstrate their ability in detecting and localizing\nL62@P3: leakage events. The teams will be given a historical SCADA dataset along with information related with the\nL63@P3: leakages detected and fixed by the utility throughout 2018, to use for training purposes and for calibrating\nL64@P3: their models. It is possible that more leakage events occurred during 2018, however the utility was not able\nL65@P3: to detect and localize them.\nL66@P3: Throughout 2019, the utility conducted periodic surveys using additional sensing equipment, pipe inspections\nL67@P3: and other methods, and was able to detect and isolate all the leakage events that occurred within that period.\nL68@P3: The most critical of these events were repaired, however it was not possible to repair some of these leakages\nL69@P3: due to financial reasons.\nL70@P3: The overall goal of this competition, is to identify methods which are able to detect and localize the leakage\nL71@P3: events that occurred in L-Town in 2019, as fast as possible (with respect to time) and as accurately as possible\nL72@P3: (with respect to their location), in order to minimize their overall financial costs, both in water losses, as well\nL73@P3: as due to the hours spent in isolating the leakage by the utility staff. The L-Town utility will compare the dif\u0002ferent solutions and select the best one based on that objective. More details regarding the evaluation proce\u0002dure will be released in January 2020.\nL74@P3: 4. DATASET\nL75@P3: The participant will be provided with the following data:\nL76@P3: 1. “2018 SCADA.xlsx” historical dataset (also in CSV): The historical data have been collected for the period\nL77@P3: 2018-01-01 00:00 until 2018-12-31 23:55, at 5-minute time steps The SCADA dataset is comprised of the\nL78@P3: water tank level, the flow sensors, the AMR measurements and the pressure sensors.\nL79@P3: 2. “2018 Leak Report.txt” (TXT): The repair times of pipe bursts that have been fixed are provided.\nL80@P3: 3. “L-Town.inp” EPANET Model (INP): A model of the network is provided with nominal parameters for all the\nL81@P3: system elements. The nominal base demands for each node are based on average historical metered con\u0002sumption. In general, the difference between the actual and the nominal values for each consumer type\nL82@P3: (residential and commercial) is less than 10%. Weekly demand profiles for two consumer types (residential\nL83@P3: and commercial) are also provided, however they do not capture the yearly seasonality. Furthermore, the\nL84@P3: EPANET model parameters may be different from the actual network parameters (e.g., diameters, rough\u0002ness coefficients), and it is assumed than in general this difference is no greater than 10% of the nominal\nL85@P3-4: values. 4. “2019 SCADA.xlsx” evaluation Dataset (also in CSV): The evaluation data have been collected for the period\nL86@P4: 2019-01-01 00:00 until 2019-12-31 23:55, at 5-minute time steps. The SCADA dataset is comprised of the\nL87@P4: water tank level, the flow sensors, the AMR measurements and the pressure sensors. This dataset will be\nL88@P4: used for the evaluation of the competing leakage detection methodologies, and will be disclosed within\nL89@P4: January 2020.\nL90@P4: Sensor details:\nL91@P4: • Three (3) flow sensors exist in the system, two at the DMA inflows and one at the pump. Measurement\nL92@P4: units: cubic meters per hour (𝑚3/ℎ).\nL93@P4: • 33 pressure sensors exist in the system which give the average pressure of the last 5 minutes at the sen\u0002sor location. The pressure sensors were placed using a methodology which maximizes the collective sen\u0002sitivity of the sensors to any possible leak. Measurement units: meters (m).\nL94@P4: • One (1) water level sensor has been installed in the tank. Measurement units: meters (m)\nL95@P4: • 82 AMRs installed in “Area C” which give the aggregated consumption from a number of consumers in the\nL96@P4: AMR area Measurement units: Liters per hour (𝐿/ℎ).\nL97@P4: 5. SCHEDULE\nL98@P4: Date Event\nL99@P4: 27/12/2019 Announcement of competition and release of 2018 dataset\nL100@P4: 20/1/2020 Update 2018 Dataset\nL101@P4: 27/1/2020 Release of the evaluation 2019 SCADA dataset\nL102@P4: 1/3/2020 Submission of the detection and isolation report by participants\nL103@P4: 1-4/9/2020 Public presentation at CCWI/WDSA 2020\nL104@P4: Development of jointly authored journal manuscript\nL105@P4: 6. HOW TO PARTICIPATE\nL106@P4: Participants will be provided a template “Results” file, in which they will be asked indicate the following infor\u0002mation:\nL107@P4: • Start time of each detected leak\nL108@P4: • Pipe ID of the detected leak\nL109@P4: Details on the evaluation procedure will be provided on a separate document.\nL110@P4: ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS\nL111@P4: • Authors SV, DE, PP and MP would like to acknowledge that part of this work has been partially funded by\nL112@P4: the European Union Horizon 2020 program under Grant Agreement No. 739551 (KIOS CoE), by the Interreg\nL113@P4: V-A Greece-Cyprus 2014-2020 program, co-financed by the European Union (ERDF) and National Funds of\nL114@P4: Greece and Cyprus under project SmartWater2020.\nL115@P4: • Authors AO and MQ would like to acknowledge that part of this work has been partially funded by the\nL116@P4: Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG).\nL117@P4: • Thanks to the ECE 807 students of the MSc in Intelligent Critical Infrastructure Systems of the University\nL118@P4-5: of Cyprus (http://www.msccis.ucy.ac.cy/) who assisted in evaluating the dataset.REFERENCES\nL119@P5: [1] Vrachimis, S. ACTUAL SYLLABUS P8 Generative AI Tools and Other External Resources 
Sometimes you may come across code, text or other helpful information online, or you may be 
able to generate it using AI tools such as ChatGPT or other Large Language Models (LLMs). In 
most cases, you will be allowed to integrate this information into your solution. However, if you 
do, you must always give the appropriate credit and citations (e.g. links) for the material you use 
(especially when you use the code and text you found online). In the case you use an LLM, you 
must say that you did so, and present the entire transcript of your ‘conversation’ with it, which 
should show what you asked and how you guided it, or were guided by it to the delivered 
solution. Your ‘conversation’ with it must be entirely yours, and sufficiently different from that 
of other students. Failure to give appropriate credit when using the work of others (whether 
human or AI) is considered plagiarism, and may lead to disciplinary action under NJIT's 
Academic Integrity policy (see below).

"Academic Integrity is the cornerstone of higher education and is central to the ideals of this course

http://www5.njit.edu/polici
OWN PAPER Abstract: This paper presents a field-oriented methodology for modeling and controlling pressure regulation, demonstrated on a real water 
­
distribution network (WDN) sector. The aim is to provide a practical and reproducible pathway from field measurements to deployable real-
­
time pressure control, addressing the gap between simulator-based numerical studies and full-scale field implementations. The study covers 
­
data-driven dynamic modeling, controller design, and pre-deployment performance assessment using real operational data. A low-order 
­
linear dynamic model with transport delay is identified directly from field measurements, combining 20 days of pressure and inflow data 
­
with step-response tests performed on an existing pressure-reducing valve (PRV). The model is validated against an independent 24-h 
­
dataset of real consumption, achieving a maximum deviation of approximately ±1 m H2O at the remote critical node, which is adequate for 
­
control-oriented modeling and controller synthesis. Based on the identified model, a digital reference signal tracking (RST) controller with 
­
a Smith predictor is designed using explicit performance specifications for settling time, overshoot, and bandwidth. The controller is evalu-
­
ated in silico using real consumption data not used during identification, providing an unbiased assessment of closed-loop performance 
­
under representative demand variability. Results show that the proposed strategy maintains pressure at the remote critical node within 
­
acceptable bounds while effectively rejecting demand-induced disturbances. Although closed-loop field implementation is beyond the scope 
­
of this study, the framework is explicitly conceived as a preparatory step

Fecha de revisión: 20 de septiembre de 2026. Versión corregida: `Milestone1_oV2.tex` y `Milestone1_oV2.pdf`. Este registro de revisión no forma parte del cuerpo de la propuesta académica.\n+\n+## Dictamen\n+\n+La propuesta es razonable para Milestone 1: presenta dos datasets existentes, fuentes identificables, cantidad y tipo de variables y dos tareas de regresión sobre presiones futuras. Los tamaños y las 119/43 señales principales son correctos. Hay errores y omisiones que sí conviene corregir: la lista de fugas de BattLeDIM solo cubre 2018; la copia de Kaggle modifica información de eventos; faltaba explicitar la diferencia entre telemetría registrada y estado físico durante ataques; y no se declaraba la asistencia de IA exigida por el syllabus.\n+\n+**No se puede certificar una entrega plenamente conforme sin adjuntar también la conversación completa de IA y revisar las instrucciones específicas de Canvas.** La revisión usa el syllabus y las explicaciones de Houle en clase; no presupone acceso a la rúbrica completa de Canvas ni aprobación del tema.\n+\n+## Cambios de contenido\n+\n+| Afirmación o elemento | Resultado de la comprobación | Tratamiento en oV2 |\n+|---|---|---|\n+| Área de investigación y experiencia con mediciones de campo y control | Coherente con el resumen y la introducción del manuscrito aportado. L-Town está basado en una red real de Chipre | Se declara simulación de ambos datasets; no se presenta como telemetría de campo |\n+| BattLeDIM: 210.240 observaciones | Verificadas 105.120 por año. Las cuatro tablas de cada año tienen timestamps idénticos, únicos y separados por 300 s | Se conserva el total y se explica el reparto anual; no se suman archivos paralelos como nuevas observaciones |\n+| BattLeDIM: 119 canales | 33 presiones + 3 caudales + 1 nivel + 82 demandas = 119; todos los nombres coinciden con los archivos | Se conserva el inventario completo |\n+| BattLeDIM: “Leakage fields (14)” | Correcto únicamente para el archivo oficial de 2018. El de 2019 tiene **23** columnas de caudales de fuga, más timestamp | Dos filas explícitas por año; ambos inventarios completos y separados de los predictores |\n+| Naturaleza de los campos de fugas | Son series de caudales simulados de fugas en m³/h, no simples etiquetas binarias ni sensores disponibles durante el pronóstico | Se describen como valores de referencia de eventos y se excluyen de X |\n+| Archivo Kaggle de fugas de 2019 | Tiene 14 campos y no coincide con el checksum del archivo oficial; el oficial tiene 23 | Se recomienda la fuente oficial para eventos y se advierte de la diferencia de versión |\n+| BattLeDIM: `PUMP_1` | Aparece en la tabla de caudales | Se aclara que es caudal, no un estado de bomba |\n+| Presiones de BattLeDIM | La documentación describe promedios de los cinco minutos anteriores, expresados como altura de presión en m | El target se describe como presión registrada/promediada futura; no como transitorio instantáneo |\n+| BATADAL: 15.027 registros | 8.761 normales + 4.177 de desarrollo mixto + 2.089 de test; verificado leyendo fuentes oficiales y mirror | Se conserva; el total no equivale a datos disponibles solo para entrenamiento |\n+| BATADAL: 43 variables | 7 niveles + 12 caudales + 12 estados + 12 presiones = 43; todos los identificadores son correctos | Se conserva; timestamp y etiqueta se cuentan aparte |\n+| BATADAL: `ATT_FLAG` | En el segundo conjunto oficial hay 3.958 valores desconocidos `-999` y 219 valores `1`. El mirror tiene 3.685 valores `0` y 492 valores `1` | Se explica que el mirror cambia etiquetas desconocidas; no se interpretan como normales oficiales |\n+| BATADAL: test con etiqueta | El test oficial tiene 44 columnas: timestamp + 43 señales; el mirror agrega `ATT_FLAG` | Se deja claro que no existe en el test original y que su presencia depende de la versión |\n+| Análisis de robustez durante ataques | Es una posibilidad metodológica, pero las lecturas pueden haber sido manipuladas | La evaluación se refiere a señales registradas; no se afirma recuperación de presión física verdadera |\n+| Nodo “crítico” o “remoto” | Es motivación del proyecto, no propiedad ya demostrada del sensor que se seleccionará | Se mantiene la selección de un nodo monitorizado como decisión pendiente de revisar topología y presión |\n+| Horizontes de 15 min / 1 h | Propuestas compatibles con las cadencias, no resultados establecidos por las fuentes | Se mantienen como objetivos de regresión; no se promete precisión |\n+| Novedad | Cambiar el objetivo del benchmark no demuestra por sí solo novedad frente a toda la literatura | Se expresa que la contribución debe establecerse en la revisión de M2 |\n+| Coherencia temporal | El original no explicita el criterio de evaluación | Se añade una frase sobre particiones ordenadas por tiempo e información disponible al pronosticar |\n+| Nombre, NJIT ID y UCID | Coinciden con los datos proporcionados por el usuario en esta conversación | Se conservan; no fueron validados contra registros privados de NJIT |\n+| Uso de IA | El PDF original no declara asistencia pese a la política expresa del syllabus | Se añade declaración factual; debe acompañarse con el transcript completo al entregar |\n+\n+## Evidencia de archivos\n+\n+Se conservaron las descargas oficiales y registros de auditoría en `build/audit_o_evidence/`. Los datos brutos no se publicaron ni se enviaron a terceros.\n+\n+- [Resultados generales, conteos, headers, checksums y registros bibliográficos](build/audit_o_evidence/audit_results.json).\n+- [Comparación numérica de BATADAL](build/audit_o_evidence/batadal_comparison.json).\n+- [Cadencia y correspondencia temporal de BattLeDIM](build/audit_o_evidence/chronology.json).\n+- [Script de descarga y auditoría](build/audit_milestone_o.py).\n+\n+Los ocho archivos SCADA de BattLeDIM (cuatro por año) coinciden **byte por byte**, mediante MD5, con los checksums publicados en Zenodo. No hay diferencias tras interpretar los números. Las diferencias se concentran en las etiquetas: 3.958 modificaciones en el segundo archivo y una columna añadida en el test. Esto no acredita la corrección de las etiquetas añadidas por Kaggle.\n+\n+## Fuentes verificadas\n+\n+1. [BATADAL: datos oficiales](https://www.batadal.net/data.html): simulación, archivos, unidades y etiquetado original.\n+6. El contenido sobre métodos se comprobó también contra el manuscrito aportado en `Proyecto`.\n+\n+Los DOI existen y corresponden a los artículos citados. El acceso automatizado al texto de ASCE no funcionó; se usaron documentación de los autores, archivos primarios y registros del editor en Crossref. No se afirma haber reproducido los resultados de esos artículos. WR-WRENG-7321_AUTHOR_CORRECTIONS (1).pdf 4105253
---
name: "pdf"
description: "Read, create, inspect, render, and verify PDF files where visual layout matters, including fillable AcroForms. Use Poppler rendering plus Python tools such as reportlab, pdfplumber, and pypdf for generation and extraction."
---

# PDF Skill

## When To Use

- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.
- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.
- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.
- Validate final rendering before delivery.

## Tools + Contract Requirements

Immediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.

```bash
node container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf
```

## Workflow

1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.
   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.
   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.
2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.
3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.
4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.

## Fill And Validate AcroForms

Visual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.

1. Keep the result interactive by default; set `flatten=True` only when the user explicitly requests a completed, static form. Preserve the source PDF, and do not flatten a signed PDF without an explicit workflow decision.
2. Inspect both representations before filling: enumerate fields from `reader.get_fields()` and `/Widget` annotations from every page's `/Annots`, following `/Parent` and `/Kids`. If a widget and a canonical field have the same name but are distinct objects with no `/Parent` relationship, do not call `reattach_fields()` blindly: it can create a second top-level field with the same name. Report the ambiguity or produce a static result.
3. Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:

```python
from pypdf import PdfReader, PdfWriter
from pypdf.generic import NameObject

reader = PdfReader(input_pdf)
writer = PdfWriter()
writer.clone_document_from_reader(reader)

# Restores widgets that are missing from /AcroForm/Fields.
writer.reattach_fields()
fields = writer.get_fields() or {}
missing = set(expected_values) - set(fields)
if missing:
    raise ValueError(f"Form fields not found after repair: {sorted(missing)}")

values_to_write = dict(expected_values)
if flatten:
    # Paint every existing value before removing every widget.
    values_to_write = {
        name: field.get("/V", "/Off" if field.get("/FT") == "/Btn" else "")
        for name, field in fields.items()
    }
    values_to_write.update(expected_values)

writer.update_page_form_field_values(
    None, values_to_write, auto_regenerate=False, flatten=flatten
)

if flatten:
    # pypdf's flatten=True paints appearances but does not remove widgets.
    writer.remove_annotations(subtypes="/Widget")
    writer.root_object.pop(NameObject("/AcroForm"), None)

with open(output_pdf, "wb") as stream:
    writer.write(stream)
```

4. Reopen the written PDF before delivery. For an interactive result, require every expected field to be present in `get_fields()` with the expected `/V`, enumerate page widgets again, and confirm their effective `/V` (the widget value or inherited `/Parent` value) agrees. Confirm each updated widget has a non-empty `/AP` `/N` appearance and render the final pages to catch stale or clipped appearances. Do not rely on `/NeedAppearances` or a successful PNG render as proof that logical field data was updated.
5. For a flattened result, require zero `/Widget` annotations and no remaining `/AcroForm` field tree after reopening, then render the final pages. Keep an editable copy when the user may need to revise the form.

## Temp And Output Conventions

- Use `tmp/pdfs/` for intermediate files; delete them when done.
- Write final artifacts under `output/pdf/` when working in this repo.
- Keep filenames stable and descriptive.

## Dependencies

Prefer the Codex bundled workspace/runtime dependencies when available. The primary runtime is expected to include:

- Python packages: `reportlab`, `pdfplumber`, `pypdf`
- Rendering tools: `pdftoppm` and `pdfinfo` from Poppler

If a dependency is missing, install only what is needed.

Python packages:

```bash
uv pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

If `uv` is unavailable:

```bash
python3 -m pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

System tools for rendering:

```bash
# macOS (Homebrew)
brew install poppler

# Ubuntu/Debian
sudo apt-get install -y poppler-utils
```

If installation is not possible in this environment, tell the user which dependency is missing and how to install it locally.

## Environment

No required environment variables.

## Rendering Command

```bash
pdftoppm -png "$INPUT_PDF" "$OUTPUT_PREFIX"
```

## Quality Expectations

- Maintain polished visual design: consistent typography, spacing, margins, and section hierarchy.
- Avoid rendering issues: clipped text, overlapping elements, broken tables, black squares, or unreadable glyphs.
- Charts, tables, and images must be sharp, aligned, and clearly labeled.
- Use ASCII hyphens only. Avoid U+2011 and other Unicode dashes.
- Citations and references must be human-readable; never leave tool tokens or placeholder strings.

## Final Checks

- Do not deliver until the latest PNG inspection shows zero visual or formatting defects.
- Confirm headers, footers, page numbering, and section transitions look polished.
- Keep intermediate files organized or remove them after final approval.

## Final response

### Final response citations

Place `:codex-file-citation{...}` inline in prose, not in a trailing list. Use `purpose="source"` for Q&amp;A/no-op and `purpose="output"` for create/edit.

- [HARD REQUIREMENT] Create/edit: cite each final PDF exactly once with a plain output citation. Summarize representative changes; do not cite every page or add a separate filename, path, or Markdown link. Example: `Created :codex-file-citation{path="/abs/path/report.pdf" purpose="output"}, with the completed analysis and appendix.`
- Q&amp;A/no-op: do not edit or re-export. Inspect the complete relevant pages, preserve material headings, table/figure labels, footnotes, sources, and sample sizes, and cite each source PDF once with a plain source citation.

PDF citations currently support only plain file citations. Do not add `artifact_kind`, `page_number`, or other locators. Never cite rendered PNGs, scratch files, builders, or QA intermediates unless asked.

### Final response suggested followups

- After successfully creating, editing, or analyzing an artifact, finish your final response with 3 useful next actions tailored to the artifact and the user's goal. Completing the requested work is not a reason to skip these actions: they help the user continue from the result. Do not perform the actions unless the user chooses one.
- Each action must be a distinct, concrete request the user could send next. Do not repeat completed work or invent filler. Omit the list if the user declines suggestions, the requested work fails, or no genuinely useful next action remains.
- Present each action as an unescaped Markdown list item using this exact syntax: `- :codex-followup[Short action]{prompt="Complete request for that action"}`.

The goal of the battle was to compare the performance of algorithms for the detection of cyber-physical attacks, whose frequency has increased in the last few years along with the adoption of smart water technologies.
